Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, honestly, it’s... it’s mostly my eating that brings me in. It’s just gotten completely off track lately. I mean, I work as a healthcare support worker, so you know, my shifts can be really demanding, twelve hours on your feet, running around helping patients, turning them, getting supplies. You really need energy for that kind of work, right? But lately, I just... I wake up and the thought of food is just, I don't know, I just can't do it. 

Some days, I barely manage to force down a cup of coffee and maybe a granola bar before a shift. And that's if I'm lucky. I used to pack these big lunches—sandwiches, fruit, you know, a proper meal—but now I don't even open my lunch bag half the time in the breakroom. I just sit there. And the thing that really made me realize I needed to come in and see someone is my clothes. My scrubs have been hanging so loose on me lately. Like, I had to pull the drawstring on my pants so tight the other day just to keep them from sliding down while I was helping transfer a patient from their bed to a wheelchair. They’re just totally loose, bagging everywhere, the top is practically swallowing me. 

And the crazy thing is, I’m not even trying to lose weight. I haven't been on a diet or exercising more or anything like that, it's just... the weight is just falling off me because I have absolutely zero interest in eating. It's like my stomach is just shut off completely, and it's starting to make getting through my work day really, really hard.

Yeah, I have. It's... it's been really bad, honestly. I don't know how else to describe it other than just this crushing weight. Like, most mornings I wake up with this heavy, hollow feeling right in the middle of my chest. It’s like the day is already completely ruined before I even put on my badge or tie my shoes. I just lie there staring at the ceiling, and the alarm is going off, and I know I need to get up because, you know, my unit relies on me. If I'm not there, they're short-staffed, and the nurses have to do the heavy lifting, and the patients don't get their baths on time. But even knowing all that, knowing people need me, I just feel so incredibly down. 

It’s not just a passing sadness, either. It’s this deep, dark cloud that just follows me everywhere. I used to love my days off. I used to go fishing down by the lake, or just tinker with my car in the driveway, you know, normal stuff. I used to look forward to the weekend so much. But now? Now I just don't care. The fishing rods are just gathering dust in the garage. I haven't even washed my car in weeks, which is so unlike me. It’s like the color has just been drained out of everything. I try to watch TV or listen to the radio on my commute, but it all just feels like noise. There’s no joy in it anymore. I just feel so empty and sad all the time, and it’s just exhausting trying to pretend I'm okay when I'm at work, putting on that fake smile for the patients when inside I just feel completely dead.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, sleep is... well, it's a whole other nightmare, honestly. I definitely don't sleep too much, it's the exact opposite. I mean, you'd think after a twelve-hour shift on my feet, wrestling with heavy equipment and helping patients all day, I'd just pass out the second my head hits the pillow. My body is completely exhausted, my back aches, my feet throb, but I get into bed and... my brain just refuses to shut off. 

My sleep is just so incredibly broken up. I just lie there in the dark, staring at the ceiling, and I start replaying the entire workday in my head. Like, did I remember to record Mrs. Gable's fluid intake? Did I lock the linens cart on the third floor? Should I have told the charge nurse about how Mr. Miller was transferring? It’s this endless, exhausting loop of everything I did or didn't do. 

And while I'm doing that, I'm also just lying there completely tense, listening for noises. Every little sound gets magnified in the quiet. The house settling, the fridge kicking on, a branch brushing the siding... I just fixate on it, waiting for the next sound, so I never really sink into a deep sleep. I just sort of doze and wake up, doze and wake up, just constantly on edge. 

But the absolute worst part is what happens a few hours later. Even if I do finally manage to string together a little bit of actual sleep, my eyes will just snap wide open. I'll look over at the clock on the nightstand, and it is always, without fail, right around 3 or 4 in the morning. Just staring at those red numbers glaring in the dark. And once I'm up at that hour, that's it. I am completely done. I try so hard to settle back down. I flip the pillow to the cool side, I try pulling the blankets up, kicking them off, trying to get comfortable, but I just can't do it. My mind is already racing again. So I just end up lying there for hours in the pitch black, agonizing over everything, just waiting and waiting for the alarm to finally go off at 5:30. By the time it actually rings, I feel worse than when I went to bed.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well... it’s hard to say out loud, but... yeah, I have. I mean, I haven't made a plan or anything like that. I'm not sitting around figuring out how to, you know, actually do something to myself. I don't have a specific method in mind or a date picked out or anything concrete like that. But... the thoughts are definitely there. They just kind of creep in, especially during those early morning hours when I'm just lying there awake at 3 AM. 

It’s more like... I just catch myself wishing I wouldn't wake up at all. Like, when I finally do drift off for those few miserable hours, part of me is just hoping that the alarm won't matter because I just won't open my eyes again. It just feels like it would be so much easier. You know? Like, if I could just disappear, just sort of fade away into nothing, then I wouldn't have to face another shift, I wouldn't have to put on that fake smile for the patients, I wouldn't have to deal with this crushing weight on my chest all day long. 

I think about it when I'm driving to work, too. I'll be sitting at a red light, watching the cars go by, and I'll just think, "What if I just kept driving? What if I just drove until I ran out of gas and then just walked away and never came back?" Or I'll think about how simple it would be if I just wasn't here anymore. Not that I want to cause my coworkers trouble or leave them short-staffed, because I know they rely on me, but the pain of just existing right now is so exhausting. It takes so much energy just to breathe sometimes. So, no, no actual plans to hurt myself, but... yeah, a lot of wishing I could just be gone. It’s just this constant, heavy thought that it would all be so much simpler if I just wasn't around to feel this way anymore.

like 'he messed up again' in the hallway or near the phlebotomy room when nobody is there, and it can happen even on days when I’m not especially sad.\n- key: S021\n  name: Disorganized_Thinking_Speech\n  description: Symptoms where speech or thought appears scattered and difficult to follow due to a lack of logical or consistent flow.\n  manifestation: When coworkers ask me a simple intake question, I can start explaining the EMR screen, then jump to staffing numbers, then to something I read for licensure, and I can see from their faces that I lost the point.\n- key: S024\n  name: Depressed_Mood\n  description: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.\n  manifestation: Most mornings I wake up with this heavy, hollow feeling like the day is already ruined before I even put on my badge.\n- key: S025\n  name: Loss_of_Interest\n  description: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.\n  manifestation: I used to run in Van Cortlandt Park, take photos, and play chess with friends, but now I mostly tell myself there’s no point and end up sitting at home scrolling or checking my budget spreadsheet.\n- key: S027\n  name: Sleep_Disturbance\n  description: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.\n  manifestation: My sleep is broken up because I lie there replaying work and listening for noises, then I wake around 3 or 4 in the morning and can’t settle back down before the alarm.\n- key: S028\n  name: Psychomotor_Changes\n  description: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.\n  manifestation: At work I either pace around the supply area checking the same drawers over and over, or I move so slowly that drawing up labels and answering a patient takes me twice as long as it should.\n- key: S029\n  name: Fatigue\n  description: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.\n  manifestation: Even after a full night in bed, my body feels like I just finished a double shift, and by lunchtime I’m fighting to keep my thoughts together.\n- key: S030\n  name: Worthlessness_Guilt\n  description: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).\n  manifestation: I keep thinking I’m a burden to the clinic and to my family, and I blame myself for small mistakes like a delayed room turnover as if I’ve harmed everyone’s whole day.\n- key: S032\n  name: Death_Suicide\n  description: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.\n  manifestation: I haven’t made a plan to hurt myself, but I catch myself wishing I just wouldn’t wake up or thinking it would be easier if I disappeared.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: psychotic_symptoms and depressive_episode occur concurrently\n  manifestation: When the depression is at its worst, the voices and the feeling that people are tracking my charting are still right there with it, so I’m fighting both at the same time during shifts.\n- key: additional_requirement_2\n  name: Additional Requirement 2\n  description: depressive_episode is present for the majority of the total duration of the active and residual portions of the illness (Criterion C)\n  manifestation: For most of this whole stretch, I’ve been living with that heavy, hollow mood and loss of interest, not just having a few bad weeks here and there.\n- key: additional_requirement_3\n  name: Additional Requirement 3\n  description: delusions_and_hallucinations occur for 2 or more weeks in the absence of a major mood episode (Criterion B)\n  manifestation: There was a period when my mood had eased up enough that I was running a little again and getting through the day, but for several weeks I still heard comments in empty hallways and kept reading the schedule like it had hidden warnings about me.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I’ve started making avoidable mistakes at work, needing coworkers to repeat simple requests, and avoiding chess nights and photography walks because I don’t trust myself to keep up or act normal.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 11 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on since the clinic’s EMR and workflow overhaul changed my hours, almost two years ago now.\n- key: psychotic_symptoms_duration\n  name: Psychotic Symptoms Duration\n  description: 1 year and 8 months\n  manifestation: The voices and the sense that people were sending signs through the schedule started a few months after the shift changes, about a year and eight months ago.\n- key: depressive_episode_duration\n  name: Depressive Episode Duration\n  description: 1 year and 4 months\n  manifestation: The steady depression and loss of interest settled in later, and it has been with me for roughly the past year and four months.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 46 / healthcare_support_worker / associates\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S026\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, depressed, or have you lost interest or pleasure in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. It's... it's been really bad, honestly. I don't know how else to describe it other than just this crushing weight. Like, most mornings I wake up with this heavy, hollow feeling right in the middle of my chest. It’s like the day is already completely ruined before I even put on my badge or tie my shoes. I just lie there staring at the ceiling, and the alarm is going off, and I know I need to get up because, you know, my unit relies on me. If I'm not there, they're short-staffed, and the nurses have to do the heavy lifting, and the patients don't get their baths on time. But even knowing all that, knowing people need me, I just feel so incredibly down. \n\nIt’s not just a passing sadness, either. It’s this deep, dark cloud that just follows me everywhere. I used to love my days off. I used to go fishing down by the lake, or just tinker with my car in the driveway, you know, normal stuff. I used to look forward to the weekend so much. But now? Now I just don't care. The fishing rods are just gathering dust in the garage. I haven't even washed my car in weeks, which is so unlike me. It’s like the color has just been drained out of everything. I try to watch TV or listen to the radio on my commute, but it all just feels like noise. There’s no joy in it anymore. I just feel so empty and sad all the time, and it’s just exhausting trying to pretend I'm okay when I'm at work, putting on that fake smile for the patients when inside I just feel completely dead.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping lately, like not being able to fall asleep, staying asleep, or maybe even sleeping too much?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sleep is... well, it's a whole other nightmare, honestly. I definitely don't sleep too much, it's the exact opposite. I mean, you'd think after a twelve-hour shift on my feet, wrestling with heavy equipment and helping patients all day, I'd just pass out the second my head hits the pillow. My body is completely exhausted, my back aches, my feet throb, but I get into bed and... my brain just refuses to shut off. \n\nMy sleep is just so incredibly broken up. I just lie there in the dark, staring at the ceiling, and I start replaying the entire workday in my head. Like, did I remember to record Mrs. Gable's fluid intake? Did I lock the linens cart on the third floor? Should I have told the charge nurse about how Mr. Miller was transferring? It’s this endless, exhausting loop of everything I did or didn't do. \n\nAnd while I'm doing that, I'm also just lying there completely tense, listening for noises. Every little sound gets magnified in the quiet. The house settling, the fridge kicking on, a branch brushing the siding... I just fixate on it, waiting for the next sound, so I never really sink into a deep sleep. I just sort of doze and wake up, doze and wake up, just constantly on edge. \n\nBut the absolute worst part is what happens a few hours later. Even if I do finally manage to string together a little bit of actual sleep, my eyes will just snap wide open. I'll look over at the clock on the nightstand, and it is always, without fail, right around 3 or 4 in the morning. Just staring at those red numbers glaring in the dark. And once I'm up at that hour, that's it. I am completely done. I try so hard to settle back down. I flip the pillow to the cool side, I try pulling the blankets up, kicking them off, trying to get comfortable, but I just can't do it. My mind is already racing again. So I just end up lying there for hours in the pitch black, agonizing over everything, just waiting and waiting for the alarm to finally go off at 5:30. By the time it actually rings, I feel worse than when I went to bed.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself or not wanting to live anymore?\n\nPatient: Well... it’s hard to say out loud, but... yeah, I have. I mean, I haven't made a plan or anything like that. I'm not sitting around figuring out how to, you know, actually do something to myself. I don't have a specific method in mind or a date picked out or anything concrete like that. But... the thoughts are definitely there. They just kind of creep in, especially during those early morning hours when I'm just lying there awake at 3 AM. \n\nIt’s more like... I just catch myself wishing I wouldn't wake up at all. Like, when I finally do drift off for those few miserable hours, part of me is just hoping that the alarm won't matter because I just won't open my eyes again. It just feels like it would be so much easier. You know? Like, if I could just disappear, just sort of fade away into nothing, then I wouldn't have to face another shift, I wouldn't have to put on that fake smile for the patients, I wouldn't have to deal with this crushing weight on my chest all day long. \n\nI think about it when I'm driving to work, too. I'll be sitting at a red light, watching the cars go by, and I'll just think, \"What if I just kept driving? What if I just drove until I ran out of gas and then just walked away and never came back?\" Or I'll think about how simple it would be if I just wasn't here anymore. Not that I want to cause my coworkers trouble or leave them short-staffed, because I know they rely on me, but the pain of just existing right now is so exhausting. It takes so much energy just to breathe sometimes. So, no, no actual plans to hurt myself, but... yeah, a lot of wishing I could just be gone. It’s just this constant, heavy thought that it would all be so much simpler if I just wasn't around to feel this way anymore.\n\n[Last Question]\nHow long have you been feeling this way? When did these feelings of sadness, loss of interest, and difficulty eating and sleeping start?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Gosh, how long... it’s hard to track sometimes, because when you work the floor, the days just sort of bleed into one another. You know, you do your shifts, you come home completely drained, and time just slips by without you really noticing. But... if I really try to pin it down, this steady depression and the loss of interest, that all settled in later. I'd say it has been with me for roughly the past year and four months. 

Yeah, about sixteen months of this. I remember at first I kept trying to rationalize it, you know? Telling myself it was just regular burnout from the unit being short-staffed, or just the physical toll of transferring heavy patients all day. I kept thinking, "I just need a long weekend, I just need to catch up on my sleep and I'll be back to normal, I'll get back to tinkering with my car." But a long weekend doesn't fix it. The weeks just kept rolling by, and that heavy, hollow feeling in my chest never went away. 

A year and four months is a really long time to carry that around every single day. It just stretches on and on, month after month of waking up at 3 AM and dreading the alarm clock. And after a while, you start to forget what it even felt like to not be exhausted and sad all the time. It kind of becomes your new normal, as awful as that sounds. You just keep waiting for the cloud to finally break, and it just... it just never does.

The weeks just kept rolling by, and that heavy, hollow feeling in my chest never went away. \n\nA year and four months is a really long time to carry that around every single day. It just stretches on and on, month after month of waking up at 3 AM and dreading the alarm clock. And after a while, you start to forget what it even felt like to not be exhausted and sad all the time. It kind of becomes your new normal, as awful as that sounds. You just keep waiting for the cloud to finally break, and it just... it just never does.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. The weeks just kept rolling by, and that heavy, hollow feeling in my chest never went away. \n\nA year and four months is a really long time to carry that around every single day. It just stretches on and on, month after month of waking up at 3 AM and dreading the alarm clock. And after a while, you start to forget what it even felt like to not be exhausted and sad all the time. It kind of becomes your new normal, as awful as that sounds. You just keep waiting for the cloud to finally break, and it just... it just never does.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever experienced periods where your mood was unusually elevated, you felt extremely energetic, or you needed less sleep but still had lots of energy?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 46 / healthcare_support_worker / associates\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Elevated? Energetic? No, no, definitely not. I mean, I wish I had periods where I felt energetic, honestly. That sounds like a dream compared to this. But no, I've never had anything like that. I've never had a time where I felt unusually happy or up, or where I was bouncing off the walls with energy. I don't think I've felt truly energetic in... well, I can't even remember. 

And needing less sleep? Like I was saying before, I *get* less sleep, but I definitely don't feel energized from it. I feel like a zombie. When I'm lying there awake at 3 AM, my body is just screaming for rest, but my brain won't let it happen. It's not like I'm waking up at that hour feeling refreshed and ready to tackle the day, like, "Oh boy, let me go clean the whole house and reorganize the garage!" It's just pure exhaustion. 

I see some of the younger nurses on the floor, you know, they work a double shift and then go out for drinks afterward, and I just look at them and think, "How on earth do you do that?" If I manage to get through my twelve hours without collapsing, it's a miracle. I drag myself to my car, I drag myself into the house, and I just collapse. There's no extra energy, no feeling "high" on life or anything like that. It's just a constant, steady drain. Just pushing through mud, every single day. So, no, nothing like what you're describing. Just this heavy, tired feeling that never goes away.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Two weeks? Oh... doc, if it was just two weeks, I think I’d be throwing a parade right now. I mean, two weeks sounds like a vacation compared to what this is. Like I was saying before about the last sixteen months or so... for most of this whole stretch, I’ve been living with that heavy, hollow mood and loss of interest. It’s not just having a few bad weeks here and there, or, you know, getting a temporary case of the blues because work is stressing me out. It's... it's every single day. 

Most of the day, nearly every day... yeah, absolutely. It’s like a thick fog that just rolled in and refused to leave. From the second I manage to force myself out of bed and put my scrubs on, it's just there. This weight. And the things I used to care about? I just couldn't care less anymore. I used to love my days off, I'd get out in the garage, I'd watch the game, I'd actually want to be awake and doing things. Now, if I have a day off, I just sit there. I don't want to do anything. I don't have the drive to do anything. It's just this constant, hollow emptiness that sits right in the middle of my chest, no matter where I am or what I'm supposed to be doing. 

So when you ask if it's been at least two weeks... I mean, I really wish I could say yes, it was just a rough couple of weeks back in October and then it cleared up and I moved on. But it hasn't cleared up. It’s just been this endless stretch of nothing, day in and day out, where everything just feels pointless and gray. It just doesn't stop.

You just keep waiting for the cloud to finally break, and it just... it just never does.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced periods where your mood was unusually elevated, you felt extremely energetic, or you needed less sleep but still had lots of energy?\n\nPatient: Elevated? Energetic? No, no, definitely not. I mean, I wish I had periods where I felt energetic, honestly. That sounds like a dream compared to this. But no, I've never had anything like that. I've never had a time where I felt unusually happy or up, or where I was bouncing off the walls with energy. I don't think I've felt truly energetic in... well, I can't even remember. \n\nAnd needing less sleep? Like I was saying before, I *get* less sleep, but I definitely don't feel energized from it. I feel like a zombie. When I'm lying there awake at 3 AM, my body is just screaming for rest, but my brain won't let it happen. It's not like I'm waking up at that hour feeling refreshed and ready to tackle the day, like, \"Oh boy, let me go clean the whole house and reorganize the garage!\" It's just pure exhaustion. \n\nI see some of the younger nurses on the floor, you know, they work a double shift and then go out for drinks afterward, and I just look at them and think, \"How on earth do you do that?\" If I manage to get through my twelve hours without collapsing, it's a miracle. I drag myself to my car, I drag myself into the house, and I just collapse. There's no extra energy, no feeling \"high\" on life or anything like that. It's just a constant, steady drain. Just pushing through mud, every single day. So, no, nothing like what you're describing. Just this heavy, tired feeling that never goes away.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting at least two weeks where you felt depressed most of the day, nearly every day, or lost interest in almost all activities?\n\nPatient: Two weeks? Oh... doc, if it was just two weeks, I think I’d be throwing a parade right now. I mean, two weeks sounds like a vacation compared to what this is. Like I was saying before about the last sixteen months or so... for most of this whole stretch, I’ve been living with that heavy, hollow mood and loss of interest. It’s not just having a few bad weeks here and there, or, you know, getting a temporary case of the blues because work is stressing me out. It's... it's every single day. \n\nMost of the day, nearly every day... yeah, absolutely. It’s like a thick fog that just rolled in and refused to leave. From the second I manage to force myself out of bed and put my scrubs on, it's just there. This weight. And the things I used to care about? I just couldn't care less anymore. I used to love my days off, I'd get out in the garage, I'd watch the game, I'd actually want to be awake and doing things. Now, if I have a day off, I just sit there. I don't want to do anything. I don't have the drive to do anything. It's just this constant, hollow emptiness that sits right in the middle of my chest, no matter where I am or what I'm supposed to be doing. \n\nSo when you ask if it's been at least two weeks... I mean, I really wish I could say yes, it was just a rough couple of weeks back in October and then it cleared up and I moved on. But it hasn't cleared up. It’s just been this endless stretch of nothing, day in and day out, where everything just feels pointless and gray. It just doesn't stop.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever experienced periods where you slept much more than usual or had an increased appetite, even if you weren’t hungry?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 46 / healthcare_support_worker / associates\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Weight_Appetite_Change\nmanifestation: \"What brings me in is that my eating has gotten so off that some days I barely get down coffee and a granola bar before a shift, and my scrubs have been hanging loose even though I’m not trying to lose weight.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Sleep more? No, definitely not sleeping more, like I mentioned earlier with the waking up at 3 AM. It's the opposite of sleeping more. And as for my appetite... well, it's funny you ask about increased appetite, because it's actually the complete opposite of that, too. That's actually a big part of what finally made me realize I needed to come in and talk to someone. My eating has just gotten so incredibly off track. 

I used to have a really hearty appetite, you know? You kind of have to when you're on your feet for a twelve-hour shift, lifting patients, running up and down the halls. I used to pack these huge lunches—sandwiches, pasta, you name it—and I'd eat every last bite in the breakroom. But now? Now, the thought of eating just... it just turns my stomach, honestly. Most days, I'm lucky if I can force myself to choke down a cup of coffee and maybe a dry granola bar before I head out the door for a shift. And even that feels like a chore. It's like my stomach is just tied in knots, or like there's a rock sitting in it, and there's just no room for food. I look at a plate of food and I just feel nothing. No hunger, no craving, just... apathy, I guess.

And it's really starting to show, too. I've noticed over the last few months that my scrubs are just hanging off me. They used to fit pretty snug, especially around the shoulders and waist, but now I'm constantly having to pull the drawstring tighter and tighter just to keep my pants up. I catch sight of myself in the mirror in the patient bathrooms sometimes, and my face just looks... gaunt. Thinner. And I haven't been trying to lose weight at all, not one bit. I just literally have zero desire to eat. So, no, definitely no increased appetite. Quite the opposite, really. It's just another thing that feels completely broken right now.

They’re just totally loose, bagging everywhere, the top is practically swallowing me. \n\nAnd the crazy thing is, I’m not even trying to lose weight. I haven't been on a diet or exercising more or anything like that, it's just... the weight is just falling off me because I have absolutely zero interest in eating. It's like my stomach is just shut off completely, and it's starting to make getting through my work day really, really hard.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, depressed, or have you lost interest or pleasure in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. I used to love my days off. I used to go fishing down by the lake, or just tinker with my car in the driveway, you know, normal stuff. I used to look forward to the weekend so much. But now? Now I just don't care. The fishing rods are just gathering dust in the garage. I haven't even washed my car in weeks, which is so unlike me. It’s like the color has just been drained out of everything. I try to watch TV or listen to the radio on my commute, but it all just feels like noise. There’s no joy in it anymore. I just feel so empty and sad all the time, and it’s just exhausting trying to pretend I'm okay when I'm at work, putting on that fake smile for the patients when inside I just feel completely dead.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping lately, like not being able to fall asleep, staying asleep, or maybe even sleeping too much?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sleep is... well, it's a whole other nightmare, honestly. I definitely don't sleep too much, it's the exact opposite. I just lie there in the dark, staring at the ceiling, and I start replaying the entire workday in my head. Like, did I remember to record Mrs. Gable's fluid intake? Did I lock the linens cart on the third floor? Should I have told the charge nurse about how Mr. Miller was transferring? It’s this endless, exhausting loop of everything I did or didn't do. \n\nAnd while I'm doing that, I'm also just lying there completely tense, listening for noises. Every little sound gets magnified in the quiet. The house settling, the fridge kicking on, a branch brushing the siding... I just fixate on it, waiting for the next sound, so I never really sink into a deep sleep. So I just end up lying there for hours in the pitch black, agonizing over everything, just waiting and waiting for the alarm to finally go off at 5:30. By the time it actually rings, I feel worse than when I went to bed.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself or not wanting to live anymore?\n\nPatient: Well... it’s hard to say out loud, but... yeah, I have. I mean, I haven't made a plan or anything like that. I'm not sitting around figuring out how to, you know, actually do something to myself. I don't have a specific method in mind or a date picked out or anything concrete like that. But... the thoughts are definitely there. They just kind of creep in, especially during those early morning hours when I'm just lying there awake at 3 AM. \n\nIt’s more like... I just catch myself wishing I wouldn't wake up at all. Like, when I finally do drift off for those few miserable hours, part of me is just hoping that the alarm won't matter because I just won't open my eyes again. It just feels like it would be so much easier. You know? Like, if I could just disappear, just sort of fade away into nothing, then I wouldn't have to face another shift, I wouldn't have to put on that fake smile for the patients, I wouldn't have to deal with this crushing weight on my chest all day long. \n\nI think about it when I'm driving to work, too. I'll be sitting at a red light, watching the cars go by, and I'll just think, \"What if I just kept driving? What if I just drove until I ran out of gas and then just walked away and never came back?\" Or I'll think about how simple it would be if I just wasn't here anymore. Not that I want to cause my coworkers trouble or leave them short-staffed, because I know they rely on me, but the pain of just existing right now is so exhausting. It takes so much energy just to breathe sometimes. So, no, no actual plans to hurt myself, but... yeah, a lot of wishing I could just be gone. It’s just this constant, heavy thought that it would all be so much simpler if I just wasn't around to feel this way anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way? When did these feelings of sadness, loss of interest, and difficulty eating and sleeping start?\n\nPatient: Gosh, how long... it’s hard to track sometimes, because when you work the floor, the days just sort of bleed into one another. The weeks just kept rolling by, and that heavy, hollow feeling in my chest never went away. \n\nA year and four months is a really long time to carry that around every single day. You know, you do your shifts, you come home completely drained, and time just slips by without you really noticing. But... if I really try to pin it down, this steady depression and the loss of interest, that all settled in later. I'd say it has been with me for roughly the past year and four months. \n\nYeah, about sixteen months of this. I remember at first I kept trying to rationalize it, you know? Telling myself it was just regular burnout from the unit being short-staffed, or just the physical toll of transferring heavy patients all day. I kept thinking, \"I just need a long weekend, I just need to catch up on my sleep and I'll be back to normal, I'll get back to tinkering with my car.\" But a long weekend doesn't fix it. The weeks just kept rolling by, and that heavy, hollow feeling in my chest never went away. \n\nA year and four months is a really long time to carry that around every single day. It just stretches on and on, month after month of waking up at 3 AM and dreading the alarm clock. And after a while, you start to forget what it even felt like to not be exhausted and sad all the time. It kind of becomes your new normal, as awful as that sounds. You just keep waiting for the cloud to finally break, and it just... it just never does.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced periods where your mood was unusually elevated, you felt extremely energetic, or you needed less sleep but still had lots of energy?\n\nPatient: Elevated? Energetic? No, no, definitely not. I mean, I wish I had periods where I felt energetic, honestly. That sounds like a dream compared to this. But no, I've never had anything like that. I've never had a time where I felt unusually happy or up, or where I was bouncing off the walls with energy. I don't think I've felt truly energetic in... well, I can't even remember. \n\nAnd needing less sleep? Like I was saying before, I *get* less sleep, but I definitely don't feel energized from it. I feel like a zombie. When I'm lying there awake at 3 AM, my body is just screaming for rest, but my brain won't let it happen. It's not like I'm waking up at that hour feeling refreshed and ready to tackle the day, like, \"Oh boy, let me go clean the whole house and reorganize the garage!\" It's just pure exhaustion. \n\nI see some of the younger nurses on the floor, you know, they work a double shift and then go out for drinks afterward, and I just look at them and think, \"How on earth do you do that?\" If I manage to get through my twelve hours without collapsing, it's a miracle. I drag myself to my car, I drag myself into the house, and I just collapse. There's no extra energy, no feeling \"high\" on life or anything like that. It's just a constant, steady drain. Just pushing through mud, every single day. So, no, nothing like what you're describing. Just this heavy, tired feeling that never goes away.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting at least two weeks where you felt depressed most of the day, nearly every day, or lost interest in almost all activities?\n\nPatient: Two weeks? Oh... doc, if it was just two weeks, I think I’d be throwing a parade right now. I mean, two weeks sounds like a vacation compared to what this is. Like I was saying before about the last sixteen months or so... for most of this whole stretch, I’ve been living with that heavy, hollow mood and loss of interest. It’s not just having a few bad weeks here and there, or, you know, getting a temporary case of the blues because work is stressing me out. It's... it's every single day. \n\nMost of the day, nearly every day... yeah, absolutely. It’s like a thick fog that just rolled in and refused to leave. From the second I manage to force myself out of bed and put my scrubs on, it's just there. This weight. And the things I used to care about? I just couldn't care less anymore. I used to love my days off, I'd get out in the garage, I'd watch the game, I'd actually want to be awake and doing things. Now, if I have a day off, I just sit there. I don't want to do anything. I don't have the drive to do anything. It's just this constant, hollow emptiness that sits right in the middle of my chest, no matter where I am or what I'm supposed to be doing. \n\nSo when you ask if it's been at least two weeks... It just doesn't stop.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced periods where you slept much more than usual or had an increased appetite, even if you weren’t hungry?\n\nPatient: Sleep more? No, definitely not sleeping more, like I mentioned earlier with the waking up at 3 AM. It's the opposite of sleeping more. And as for my appetite... well, it's funny you ask about increased appetite, because it's actually the complete opposite of that, too. That's actually a big part of what finally made me realize I needed to come in and talk to someone. My eating has just gotten so incredibly off track. \n\nI used to have a really hearty appetite, you know? You kind of have to when you're on your feet for a twelve-hour shift, lifting patients, running up and down the halls. I used to pack these huge lunches—sandwiches, pasta, you name it—and I'd eat every last bite in the breakroom. But now? Now, the thought of eating just... it just turns my stomach, honestly. Most days, I'm lucky if I can force myself to choke down a cup of coffee and maybe a dry granola bar before I head out the door for a shift. And even that feels like a chore. It's like my stomach is just tied in knots, or like there's a rock sitting in it, and there's just no room for food. I look at a plate of food and I just feel nothing. No hunger, no craving, just... apathy, I guess.\n\nAnd it's really starting to show, too. I've noticed over the last few months that my scrubs are just hanging off me. They used to fit pretty snug, especially around the shoulders and waist, but now I'm constantly having to pull the drawstring tighter and tighter just to keep my pants up. I catch sight of myself in the mirror in the patient bathrooms sometimes, and my face just looks... gaunt. Thinner. And I haven't been trying to lose weight at all, not one bit. I just literally have zero desire to eat. So, no, definitely no increased appetite. Quite the opposite, really. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.2, F43.22\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, it’s... it’s mostly my eating that brings me in. It’s just gotten completely off track lately. I mean, I work as a healthcare support worker, so you know, my shifts can be really demanding, twelve hours on your feet, running around helping patients, turning them, getting supplies. You really need energy for that kind of work, right? But lately, I just... I wake up and the thought of food is just, I don't know, I just can't do it. \n\nSome days, I barely manage to force down a cup of coffee and maybe a granola bar before a shift. And that's if I'm lucky. I used to pack these big lunches—sandwiches, fruit, you know, a proper meal—but now I don't even open my lunch bag half the time in the breakroom. I just sit there. And the thing that really made me realize I needed to come in and see someone is my clothes. My scrubs have been hanging so loose on me lately. Like, I had to pull the drawstring on my pants so tight the other day just to keep them from sliding down while I was helping transfer a patient from their bed to a wheelchair. They’re just totally loose, bagging everywhere, the top is practically swallowing me. \n\nAnd the crazy thing is, I’m not even trying to lose weight. I haven't been on a diet or exercising more or anything like that, it's just... the weight is just falling off me because I have absolutely zero interest in eating. It's like my stomach is just shut off completely, and it's starting to make getting through my work day really, really hard.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, depressed, or have you lost interest or pleasure in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. It's... it's been really bad, honestly. I don't know how else to describe it other than just this crushing weight. Like, most mornings I wake up with this heavy, hollow feeling right in the middle of my chest. It’s like the day is already completely ruined before I even put on my badge or tie my shoes. I just lie there staring at the ceiling, and the alarm is going off, and I know I need to get up because, you know, my unit relies on me. If I'm not there, they're short-staffed, and the nurses have to do the heavy lifting, and the patients don't get their baths on time. But even knowing all that, knowing people need me, I just feel so incredibly down. \n\nIt’s not just a passing sadness, either. It’s this deep, dark cloud that just follows me everywhere. I used to love my days off. I used to go fishing down by the lake, or just tinker with my car in the driveway, you know, normal stuff. I used to look forward to the weekend so much. But now? Now I just don't care. The fishing rods are just gathering dust in the garage. I haven't even washed my car in weeks, which is so unlike me. It’s like the color has just been drained out of everything. I try to watch TV or listen to the radio on my commute, but it all just feels like noise. There’s no joy in it anymore. I just feel so empty and sad all the time, and it’s just exhausting trying to pretend I'm okay when I'm at work, putting on that fake smile for the patients when inside I just feel completely dead.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping lately, like not being able to fall asleep, staying asleep, or maybe even sleeping too much?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sleep is... well, it's a whole other nightmare, honestly. I definitely don't sleep too much, it's the exact opposite. I mean, you'd think after a twelve-hour shift on my feet, wrestling with heavy equipment and helping patients all day, I'd just pass out the second my head hits the pillow. My body is completely exhausted, my back aches, my feet throb, but I get into bed and... my brain just refuses to shut off. \n\nMy sleep is just so incredibly broken up. I just lie there in the dark, staring at the ceiling, and I start replaying the entire workday in my head. Like, did I remember to record Mrs. Gable's fluid intake? Did I lock the linens cart on the third floor? Should I have told the charge nurse about how Mr. Miller was transferring? It’s this endless, exhausting loop of everything I did or didn't do. \n\nAnd while I'm doing that, I'm also just lying there completely tense, listening for noises. Every little sound gets magnified in the quiet. The house settling, the fridge kicking on, a branch brushing the siding... I just fixate on it, waiting for the next sound, so I never really sink into a deep sleep. I just sort of doze and wake up, doze and wake up, just constantly on edge. \n\nBut the absolute worst part is what happens a few hours later. Even if I do finally manage to string together a little bit of actual sleep, my eyes will just snap wide open. I'll look over at the clock on the nightstand, and it is always, without fail, right around 3 or 4 in the morning. Just staring at those red numbers glaring in the dark. And once I'm up at that hour, that's it. I am completely done. I try so hard to settle back down. I flip the pillow to the cool side, I try pulling the blankets up, kicking them off, trying to get comfortable, but I just can't do it. My mind is already racing again. So I just end up lying there for hours in the pitch black, agonizing over everything, just waiting and waiting for the alarm to finally go off at 5:30. By the time it actually rings, I feel worse than when I went to bed.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself or not wanting to live anymore?\n\nPatient: Well... it’s hard to say out loud, but... yeah, I have. I mean, I haven't made a plan or anything like that. I'm not sitting around figuring out how to, you know, actually do something to myself. I don't have a specific method in mind or a date picked out or anything concrete like that. But... the thoughts are definitely there. They just kind of creep in, especially during those early morning hours when I'm just lying there awake at 3 AM. \n\nIt’s more like... I just catch myself wishing I wouldn't wake up at all. Like, when I finally do drift off for those few miserable hours, part of me is just hoping that the alarm won't matter because I just won't open my eyes again. It just feels like it would be so much easier. You know? Like, if I could just disappear, just sort of fade away into nothing, then I wouldn't have to face another shift, I wouldn't have to put on that fake smile for the patients, I wouldn't have to deal with this crushing weight on my chest all day long. \n\nI think about it when I'm driving to work, too. I'll be sitting at a red light, watching the cars go by, and I'll just think, \"What if I just kept driving? What if I just drove until I ran out of gas and then just walked away and never came back?\" Or I'll think about how simple it would be if I just wasn't here anymore. Not that I want to cause my coworkers trouble or leave them short-staffed, because I know they rely on me, but the pain of just existing right now is so exhausting. It takes so much energy just to breathe sometimes. So, no, no actual plans to hurt myself, but... yeah, a lot of wishing I could just be gone. It’s just this constant, heavy thought that it would all be so much simpler if I just wasn't around to feel this way anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way? When did these feelings of sadness, loss of interest, and difficulty eating and sleeping start?\n\nPatient: Gosh, how long... it’s hard to track sometimes, because when you work the floor, the days just sort of bleed into one another. You know, you do your shifts, you come home completely drained, and time just slips by without you really noticing. But... if I really try to pin it down, this steady depression and the loss of interest, that all settled in later. I'd say it has been with me for roughly the past year and four months. \n\nYeah, about sixteen months of this. I remember at first I kept trying to rationalize it, you know? Telling myself it was just regular burnout from the unit being short-staffed, or just the physical toll of transferring heavy patients all day. I kept thinking, \"I just need a long weekend, I just need to catch up on my sleep and I'll be back to normal, I'll get back to tinkering with my car.\" But a long weekend doesn't fix it. The weeks just kept rolling by, and that heavy, hollow feeling in my chest never went away. \n\nA year and four months is a really long time to carry that around every single day. It just stretches on and on, month after month of waking up at 3 AM and dreading the alarm clock. And after a while, you start to forget what it even felt like to not be exhausted and sad all the time. It kind of becomes your new normal, as awful as that sounds. You just keep waiting for the cloud to finally break, and it just... it just never does.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced periods where your mood was unusually elevated, you felt extremely energetic, or you needed less sleep but still had lots of energy?\n\nPatient: Elevated? Energetic? No, no, definitely not. I mean, I wish I had periods where I felt energetic, honestly. That sounds like a dream compared to this. But no, I've never had anything like that. I've never had a time where I felt unusually happy or up, or where I was bouncing off the walls with energy. I don't think I've felt truly energetic in... well, I can't even remember. \n\nAnd needing less sleep? Like I was saying before, I *get* less sleep, but I definitely don't feel energized from it. I feel like a zombie. When I'm lying there awake at 3 AM, my body is just screaming for rest, but my brain won't let it happen. It's not like I'm waking up at that hour feeling refreshed and ready to tackle the day, like, \"Oh boy, let me go clean the whole house and reorganize the garage!\" It's just pure exhaustion. \n\nI see some of the younger nurses on the floor, you know, they work a double shift and then go out for drinks afterward, and I just look at them and think, \"How on earth do you do that?\" If I manage to get through my twelve hours without collapsing, it's a miracle. I drag myself to my car, I drag myself into the house, and I just collapse. There's no extra energy, no feeling \"high\" on life or anything like that. It's just a constant, steady drain. Just pushing through mud, every single day. So, no, nothing like what you're describing. Just this heavy, tired feeling that never goes away.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting at least two weeks where you felt depressed most of the day, nearly every day, or lost interest in almost all activities?\n\nPatient: Two weeks? Oh... doc, if it was just two weeks, I think I’d be throwing a parade right now. I mean, two weeks sounds like a vacation compared to what this is. Like I was saying before about the last sixteen months or so... for most of this whole stretch, I’ve been living with that heavy, hollow mood and loss of interest. It’s not just having a few bad weeks here and there, or, you know, getting a temporary case of the blues because work is stressing me out. It's... it's every single day. \n\nMost of the day, nearly every day... yeah, absolutely. It’s like a thick fog that just rolled in and refused to leave. From the second I manage to force myself out of bed and put my scrubs on, it's just there. This weight. And the things I used to care about? I just couldn't care less anymore. I used to love my days off, I'd get out in the garage, I'd watch the game, I'd actually want to be awake and doing things. Now, if I have a day off, I just sit there. I don't want to do anything. I don't have the drive to do anything. It's just this constant, hollow emptiness that sits right in the middle of my chest, no matter where I am or what I'm supposed to be doing. \n\nSo when you ask if it's been at least two weeks... I mean, I really wish I could say yes, it was just a rough couple of weeks back in October and then it cleared up and I moved on. But it hasn't cleared up. It’s just been this endless stretch of nothing, day in and day out, where everything just feels pointless and gray. It just doesn't stop.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced periods where you slept much more than usual or had an increased appetite, even if you weren’t hungry?\n\nPatient: Sleep more? No, definitely not sleeping more, like I mentioned earlier with the waking up at 3 AM. It's the opposite of sleeping more. And as for my appetite... well, it's funny you ask about increased appetite, because it's actually the complete opposite of that, too. That's actually a big part of what finally made me realize I needed to come in and talk to someone. My eating has just gotten so incredibly off track. \n\nI used to have a really hearty appetite, you know? You kind of have to when you're on your feet for a twelve-hour shift, lifting patients, running up and down the halls. I used to pack these huge lunches—sandwiches, pasta, you name it—and I'd eat every last bite in the breakroom. But now? Now, the thought of eating just... it just turns my stomach, honestly. Most days, I'm lucky if I can force myself to choke down a cup of coffee and maybe a dry granola bar before I head out the door for a shift. And even that feels like a chore. It's like my stomach is just tied in knots, or like there's a rock sitting in it, and there's just no room for food. I look at a plate of food and I just feel nothing. No hunger, no craving, just... apathy, I guess.\n\nAnd it's really starting to show, too. I've noticed over the last few months that my scrubs are just hanging off me. They used to fit pretty snug, especially around the shoulders and waist, but now I'm constantly having to pull the drawstring tighter and tighter just to keep my pants up. I catch sight of myself in the mirror in the patient bathrooms sometimes, and my face just looks... gaunt. Thinner. And I haven't been trying to lose weight at all, not one bit. I just literally have zero desire to eat. So, no, definitely no increased appetite. Quite the opposite, really. It's just another thing that feels completely broken right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant weight loss recently, even though you haven’t been dieting or trying to lose weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly, like I was just saying a second ago. That's honestly the main thing that brings me in today, because the weight loss is getting impossible to ignore. I haven't actually stepped on a scale recently because I don't even own one, but it's definitely significant. \n\nLike I said, my eating has just gotten so far off track that some days I barely get down a cup of coffee and a granola bar before a whole twelve-hour shift. That's literally all I can manage to force down. And when you're working on your feet all day on the floor, lifting people and moving heavy equipment, you burn a lot of energy, but I'm putting absolutely nothing back into the tank. So yeah, my scrubs have just been hanging completely loose on me. I'm having to tie the drawstrings so tight that the fabric bunches up all weird around my waist, and even then I'm pulling them up all day. \n\nAnd I want to be really clear about this—I am not trying to lose weight at all. I've never been on a diet in my life, I've always been a pretty solid guy, you know? I'm not trying to slim down or watch my carbs or anything like that. The weight is just falling off me all on its own because my stomach just won't accept food anymore. It's just melting off, and I’m not doing a single thing to try and make it happen.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Concentrating? No... no, I wouldn't say that. It's actually kind of funny you ask that, because with how bad everything else is—the not sleeping, the weight falling off me, that constant heavy feeling I was telling you about—you'd think my brain would just completely turn to mush, right? I mean, working on the floor at the hospital, you really can't afford to get foggy or lose your concentration. It's a safety issue. If I'm doing a two-person assist, or getting a fall-risk patient up to the commode, I have to be completely dialed in. I have to remember exactly what the charge nurse told me during handoff, like which side is the patient's weak side, or where their IV lines and catheters are so nothing gets pulled out. And I haven't messed any of that up. Not once. I'm still tracking everything perfectly. 

Even with the lack of sleep and the fact that I'm barely running on a single granola bar, my brain just pushes through the mechanics of the day. I can remember that the guy in room 412 needs his ice water refilled, or that the bed alarm in 410 is super sensitive and needs to be reset a specific way. It's not foggy at all up there. If anything, I'm almost hyper-aware of everything, especially of how miserable I feel doing it all. But the actual thinking part? Following instructions, finishing my rounds, paying attention to what people are saying to me? That's all completely intact. I just do the work, even if I don't care about it one bit anymore. So, yeah, no trouble focusing or thinking clearly. The engine is still running fine, I just... I just don't want to be driving the car, if that makes sense.

I flip the pillow to the cool side, I try pulling the blankets up, kicking them off, trying to get comfortable, but I just can't do it. My mind is already racing again. So I just end up lying there for hours in the pitch black, agonizing over everything, just waiting and waiting for the alarm to finally go off at 5:30. By the time it actually rings, I feel worse than when I went to bed.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself or not wanting to live anymore?\n\nPatient: Well... it’s hard to say out loud, but... yeah, I have. I mean, I haven't made a plan or anything like that. I'm not sitting around figuring out how to, you know, actually do something to myself. I don't have a specific method in mind or a date picked out or anything concrete like that. But... the thoughts are definitely there. They just kind of creep in, especially during those early morning hours when I'm just lying there awake at 3 AM. \n\nIt’s more like... I just catch myself wishing I wouldn't wake up at all. Like, when I finally do drift off for those few miserable hours, part of me is just hoping that the alarm won't matter because I just won't open my eyes again. It just feels like it would be so much easier. You know? Like, if I could just disappear, just sort of fade away into nothing, then I wouldn't have to face another shift, I wouldn't have to put on that fake smile for the patients, I wouldn't have to deal with this crushing weight on my chest all day long. \n\nI think about it when I'm driving to work, too. I'll be sitting at a red light, watching the cars go by, and I'll just think, \"What if I just kept driving? What if I just drove until I ran out of gas and then just walked away and never came back?\" Or I'll think about how simple it would be if I just wasn't here anymore. Not that I want to cause my coworkers trouble or leave them short-staffed, because I know they rely on me, but the pain of just existing right now is so exhausting. It takes so much energy just to breathe sometimes. So, no, no actual plans to hurt myself, but... yeah, a lot of wishing I could just be gone. It’s just this constant, heavy thought that it would all be so much simpler if I just wasn't around to feel this way anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way? When did these feelings of sadness, loss of interest, and difficulty eating and sleeping start?\n\nPatient: Gosh, how long... it’s hard to track sometimes, because when you work the floor, the days just sort of bleed into one another. You know, you do your shifts, you come home completely drained, and time just slips by without you really noticing. But... if I really try to pin it down, this steady depression and the loss of interest, that all settled in later. I'd say it has been with me for roughly the past year and four months. \n\nYeah, about sixteen months of this. I remember at first I kept trying to rationalize it, you know? Telling myself it was just regular burnout from the unit being short-staffed, or just the physical toll of transferring heavy patients all day. I kept thinking, \"I just need a long weekend, I just need to catch up on my sleep and I'll be back to normal, I'll get back to tinkering with my car.\" But a long weekend doesn't fix it. The weeks just kept rolling by, and that heavy, hollow feeling in my chest never went away. \n\nA year and four months is a really long time to carry that around every single day. It just stretches on and on, month after month of waking up at 3 AM and dreading the alarm clock. And after a while, you start to forget what it even felt like to not be exhausted and sad all the time. It kind of becomes your new normal, as awful as that sounds. You just keep waiting for the cloud to finally break, and it just... it just never does.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced periods where your mood was unusually elevated, you felt extremely energetic, or you needed less sleep but still had lots of energy?\n\nPatient: Elevated? Energetic? No, no, definitely not. I mean, I wish I had periods where I felt energetic, honestly. That sounds like a dream compared to this. But no, I've never had anything like that. I've never had a time where I felt unusually happy or up, or where I was bouncing off the walls with energy. I don't think I've felt truly energetic in... well, I can't even remember. \n\nAnd needing less sleep? Like I was saying before, I *get* less sleep, but I definitely don't feel energized from it. I feel like a zombie. When I'm lying there awake at 3 AM, my body is just screaming for rest, but my brain won't let it happen. It's not like I'm waking up at that hour feeling refreshed and ready to tackle the day, like, \"Oh boy, let me go clean the whole house and reorganize the garage!\" It's just pure exhaustion. \n\nI see some of the younger nurses on the floor, you know, they work a double shift and then go out for drinks afterward, and I just look at them and think, \"How on earth do you do that?\" If I manage to get through my twelve hours without collapsing, it's a miracle. I drag myself to my car, I drag myself into the house, and I just collapse. There's no extra energy, no feeling \"high\" on life or anything like that. It's just a constant, steady drain. Just pushing through mud, every single day. So, no, nothing like what you're describing. Just this heavy, tired feeling that never goes away.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting at least two weeks where you felt depressed most of the day, nearly every day, or lost interest in almost all activities?\n\nPatient: Two weeks? Oh... doc, if it was just two weeks, I think I’d be throwing a parade right now. I mean, two weeks sounds like a vacation compared to what this is. Like I was saying before about the last sixteen months or so... for most of this whole stretch, I’ve been living with that heavy, hollow mood and loss of interest. It’s not just having a few bad weeks here and there, or, you know, getting a temporary case of the blues because work is stressing me out. It's... it's every single day. \n\nMost of the day, nearly every day... yeah, absolutely. It’s like a thick fog that just rolled in and refused to leave. From the second I manage to force myself out of bed and put my scrubs on, it's just there. This weight. And the things I used to care about? I just couldn't care less anymore. I used to love my days off, I'd get out in the garage, I'd watch the game, I'd actually want to be awake and doing things. Now, if I have a day off, I just sit there. I don't want to do anything. I don't have the drive to do anything. It's just this constant, hollow emptiness that sits right in the middle of my chest, no matter where I am or what I'm supposed to be doing. \n\nSo when you ask if it's been at least two weeks... I mean, I really wish I could say yes, it was just a rough couple of weeks back in October and then it cleared up and I moved on. But it hasn't cleared up. It’s just been this endless stretch of nothing, day in and day out, where everything just feels pointless and gray. It just doesn't stop.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced periods where you slept much more than usual or had an increased appetite, even if you weren’t hungry?\n\nPatient: Sleep more? No, definitely not sleeping more, like I mentioned earlier with the waking up at 3 AM. It's the opposite of sleeping more. And as for my appetite... well, it's funny you ask about increased appetite, because it's actually the complete opposite of that, too. That's actually a big part of what finally made me realize I needed to come in and talk to someone. My eating has just gotten so incredibly off track. \n\nI used to have a really hearty appetite, you know? You kind of have to when you're on your feet for a twelve-hour shift, lifting patients, running up and down the halls. I used to pack these huge lunches—sandwiches, pasta, you name it—and I'd eat every last bite in the breakroom. But now? Now, the thought of eating just... it just turns my stomach, honestly. Most days, I'm lucky if I can force myself to choke down a cup of coffee and maybe a dry granola bar before I head out the door for a shift. And even that feels like a chore. It's like my stomach is just tied in knots, or like there's a rock sitting in it, and there's just no room for food. I look at a plate of food and I just feel nothing. No hunger, no craving, just... apathy, I guess.\n\nAnd it's really starting to show, too. I've noticed over the last few months that my scrubs are just hanging off me. They used to fit pretty snug, especially around the shoulders and waist, but now I'm constantly having to pull the drawstring tighter and tighter just to keep my pants up. I catch sight of myself in the mirror in the patient bathrooms sometimes, and my face just looks... gaunt. Thinner. And I haven't been trying to lose weight at all, not one bit. I just literally have zero desire to eat. So, no, definitely no increased appetite. Quite the opposite, really. It's just another thing that feels completely broken right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant weight loss recently, even though you haven’t been dieting or trying to lose weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly, like I was just saying a second ago. That's honestly the main thing that brings me in today, because the weight loss is getting impossible to ignore. I haven't actually stepped on a scale recently because I don't even own one, but it's definitely significant. \n\nLike I said, my eating has just gotten so far off track that some days I barely get down a cup of coffee and a granola bar before a whole twelve-hour shift. That's literally all I can manage to force down. And when you're working on your feet all day on the floor, lifting people and moving heavy equipment, you burn a lot of energy, but I'm putting absolutely nothing back into the tank. So yeah, my scrubs have just been hanging completely loose on me. I'm having to tie the drawstrings so tight that the fabric bunches up all weird around my waist, and even then I'm pulling them up all day. \n\nAnd I want to be really clear about this—I am not trying to lose weight at all. I've never been on a diet in my life, I've always been a pretty solid guy, you know? I'm not trying to slim down or watch my carbs or anything like that. The weight is just falling off me all on its own because my stomach just won't accept food anymore. It's just melting off, and I’m not doing a single thing to try and make it happen.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating or thinking clearly lately, like your mind feels foggy or you're struggling to focus on tasks?\n\nPatient: Concentrating? No... no, I wouldn't say that. It's actually kind of funny you ask that, because with how bad everything else is—the not sleeping, the weight falling off me, that constant heavy feeling I was telling you about—you'd think my brain would just completely turn to mush, right? But honestly, my focus is fine. My mind is probably the only thing that still feels like it's working the way it's supposed to. \n\nI mean, working on the floor at the hospital, you really can't afford to get foggy or lose your concentration. It's a safety issue. If I'm doing a two-person assist, or getting a fall-risk patient up to the commode, I have to be completely dialed in. I have to remember exactly what the charge nurse told me during handoff, like which side is the patient's weak side, or where their IV lines and catheters are so nothing gets pulled out. And I haven't messed any of that up. Not once. I'm still tracking everything perfectly. \n\nEven with the lack of sleep and the fact that I'm barely running on a single granola bar, my brain just pushes through the mechanics of the day. I can remember that the guy in room 412 needs his ice water refilled, or that the bed alarm in 410 is super sensitive and needs to be reset a specific way. It's not foggy at all up there. If anything, I'm almost hyper-aware of everything, especially of how miserable I feel doing it all. But the actual thinking part? Following instructions, finishing my rounds, paying attention to what people are saying to me? That's all completely intact. I just do the work, even if I don't care about it one bit anymore. So, yeah, no trouble focusing or thinking clearly. The engine is still running fine, I just... I just don't want to be driving the car, if that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I think... wait, didn't you just ask me that? About concentrating and thinking clearly? Because, yeah, like I was just saying, my focus is actually totally fine. Making decisions, thinking clearly, focusing on tasks—it's all the same as it's always been. 

I know it probably seems strange, especially since I just told you how I'm basically running on fumes, skipping meals, and waking up at 3 AM staring at the ceiling for hours. You'd think my brain would be completely fried, right? Like I wouldn't be able to put two sentences together or figure out what to do next. But honestly, making decisions hasn't been an issue at all. Like at work, if a patient suddenly starts sliding down in their chair and I have to decide in a split second whether to grab them by the gait belt or call for backup, my brain just snaps to it. I make the call instantly. There's no hesitation, no second-guessing myself, no standing there looking confused while someone falls on the floor. 

And it's the same thing with focusing on tasks. Whether I'm charting my vitals, emptying drains, or just figuring out the most efficient way to get through my morning rounds before breakfast trays arrive, I can map it all out in my head perfectly. I don't get sidetracked. I don't walk into a room and forget why I went in there. I'm just... I'm still sharp. The gears are turning exactly the way they're supposed to. 

It's just that the whole time I'm doing these tasks, making these decisions, I'm just feeling this absolute, crushing emptiness. Like, I know *how* to do everything, and I *can* do everything, but I just don't see the point anymore. But the actual ability to think? To decide what needs to be done and focus on doing it? No, no problems there. Like I said, it's probably the only part of me that isn't falling apart right now.

I used to love my days off. I used to go fishing down by the lake, or just tinker with my car in the driveway, you know, normal stuff. I used to look forward to the weekend so much. But now? Now I just don't care. The fishing rods are just gathering dust in the garage. I haven't even washed my car in weeks, which is so unlike me. It’s like the color has just been drained out of everything. I try to watch TV or listen to the radio on my commute, but it all just feels like noise. There’s no joy in it anymore. I just feel so empty and sad all the time, and it’s just exhausting trying to pretend I'm okay when I'm at work, putting on that fake smile for the patients when inside I just feel completely dead.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping lately, like not being able to fall asleep, staying asleep, or maybe even sleeping too much?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sleep is... well, it's a whole other nightmare, honestly. I definitely don't sleep too much, it's the exact opposite. I mean, you'd think after a twelve-hour shift on my feet, wrestling with heavy equipment and helping patients all day, I'd just pass out the second my head hits the pillow. My body is completely exhausted, my back aches, my feet throb, but I get into bed and... my brain just refuses to shut off. \n\nMy sleep is just so incredibly broken up. I just lie there in the dark, staring at the ceiling, and I start replaying the entire workday in my head. Like, did I remember to record Mrs. Gable's fluid intake? Did I lock the linens cart on the third floor? Should I have told the charge nurse about how Mr. Miller was transferring? It’s this endless, exhausting loop of everything I did or didn't do. \n\nAnd while I'm doing that, I'm also just lying there completely tense, listening for noises. Every little sound gets magnified in the quiet. The house settling, the fridge kicking on, a branch brushing the siding... I just fixate on it, waiting for the next sound, so I never really sink into a deep sleep. I just sort of doze and wake up, doze and wake up, just constantly on edge. \n\nBut the absolute worst part is what happens a few hours later. Even if I do finally manage to string together a little bit of actual sleep, my eyes will just snap wide open. I'll look over at the clock on the nightstand, and it is always, without fail, right around 3 or 4 in the morning. Just staring at those red numbers glaring in the dark. And once I'm up at that hour, that's it. I am completely done. I try so hard to settle back down. I flip the pillow to the cool side, I try pulling the blankets up, kicking them off, trying to get comfortable, but I just can't do it. My mind is already racing again. So I just end up lying there for hours in the pitch black, agonizing over everything, just waiting and waiting for the alarm to finally go off at 5:30. By the time it actually rings, I feel worse than when I went to bed.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself or not wanting to live anymore?\n\nPatient: Well... it’s hard to say out loud, but... yeah, I have.
