It just feels like it would be so much easier. You know? Like, if I could just disappear, just sort of fade away into nothing, then I wouldn't have to face another shift, I wouldn't have to put on that fake smile for the patients, I wouldn't have to deal with this crushing weight on my chest all day long. \n\nI think about it when I'm driving to work, too. I'll be sitting at a red light, watching the cars go by, and I'll just think, \"What if I just kept driving? What if I just drove until I ran out of gas and then just walked away and never came back?\" Or I'll think about how simple it would be if I just wasn't here anymore. Not that I want to cause my coworkers trouble or leave them short-staffed, because I know they rely on me, but the pain of just existing right now is so exhausting. It takes so much energy just to breathe sometimes. So, no, no actual plans to hurt myself, but... yeah, a lot of wishing I could just be gone. It’s just this constant, heavy thought that it would all be so much simpler if I just wasn't around to feel this way anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way? When did these feelings of sadness, loss of interest, and difficulty eating and sleeping start?\n\nPatient: Gosh, how long... it’s hard to track sometimes, because when you work the floor, the days just sort of bleed into one another. You know, you do your shifts, you come home completely drained, and time just slips by without you really noticing. But... if I really try to pin it down, this steady depression and the loss of interest, that all settled in later. I'd say it has been with me for roughly the past year and four months. \n\nYeah, about sixteen months of this. I remember at first I kept trying to rationalize it, you know? Telling myself it was just regular burnout from the unit being short-staffed, or just the physical toll of transferring heavy patients all day. I kept thinking, \"I just need a long weekend, I just need to catch up on my sleep and I'll be back to normal, I'll get back to tinkering with my car.\" But a long weekend doesn't fix it. The weeks just kept rolling by, and that heavy, hollow feeling in my chest never went away. \n\nA year and four months is a really long time to carry that around every single day. It just stretches on and on, month after month of waking up at 3 AM and dreading the alarm clock. And after a while, you start to forget what it even felt like to not be exhausted and sad all the time. It kind of becomes your new normal, as awful as that sounds. You just keep waiting for the cloud to finally break, and it just... it just never does.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced periods where your mood was unusually elevated, you felt extremely energetic, or you needed less sleep but still had lots of energy?\n\nPatient: Elevated? Energetic? No, no, definitely not. I mean, I wish I had periods where I felt energetic, honestly. That sounds like a dream compared to this. But no, I've never had anything like that. I've never had a time where I felt unusually happy or up, or where I was bouncing off the walls with energy. I don't think I've felt truly energetic in... well, I can't even remember. \n\nAnd needing less sleep? Like I was saying before, I *get* less sleep, but I definitely don't feel energized from it. I feel like a zombie. When I'm lying there awake at 3 AM, my body is just screaming for rest, but my brain won't let it happen. It's not like I'm waking up at that hour feeling refreshed and ready to tackle the day, like, \"Oh boy, let me go clean the whole house and reorganize the garage!\" It's just pure exhaustion. \n\nI see some of the younger nurses on the floor, you know, they work a double shift and then go out for drinks afterward, and I just look at them and think, \"How on earth do you do that?\" If I manage to get through my twelve hours without collapsing, it's a miracle. I drag myself to my car, I drag myself into the house, and I just collapse. There's no extra energy, no feeling \"high\" on life or anything like that. It's just a constant, steady drain. Just pushing through mud, every single day. So, no, nothing like what you're describing. Just this heavy, tired feeling that never goes away.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting at least two weeks where you felt depressed most of the day, nearly every day, or lost interest in almost all activities?\n\nPatient: Two weeks? Oh... doc, if it was just two weeks, I think I’d be throwing a parade right now. I mean, two weeks sounds like a vacation compared to what this is. Like I was saying before about the last sixteen months or so... for most of this whole stretch, I’ve been living with that heavy, hollow mood and loss of interest. It’s not just having a few bad weeks here and there, or, you know, getting a temporary case of the blues because work is stressing me out. It's... it's every single day. \n\nMost of the day, nearly every day... yeah, absolutely. It’s like a thick fog that just rolled in and refused to leave. From the second I manage to force myself out of bed and put my scrubs on, it's just there. This weight. And the things I used to care about? I just couldn't care less anymore. I used to love my days off, I'd get out in the garage, I'd watch the game, I'd actually want to be awake and doing things. Now, if I have a day off, I just sit there. I don't want to do anything. I don't have the drive to do anything. It's just this constant, hollow emptiness that sits right in the middle of my chest, no matter where I am or what I'm supposed to be doing. \n\nSo when you ask if it's been at least two weeks... I mean, I really wish I could say yes, it was just a rough couple of weeks back in October and then it cleared up and I moved on. But it hasn't cleared up. It’s just been this endless stretch of nothing, day in and day out, where everything just feels pointless and gray. It just doesn't stop.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced periods where you slept much more than usual or had an increased appetite, even if you weren’t hungry?\n\nPatient: Sleep more? No, definitely not sleeping more, like I mentioned earlier with the waking up at 3 AM. It's the opposite of sleeping more. And as for my appetite... well, it's funny you ask about increased appetite, because it's actually the complete opposite of that, too. That's actually a big part of what finally made me realize I needed to come in and talk to someone. My eating has just gotten so incredibly off track. \n\nI used to have a really hearty appetite, you know? You kind of have to when you're on your feet for a twelve-hour shift, lifting patients, running up and down the halls. I used to pack these huge lunches—sandwiches, pasta, you name it—and I'd eat every last bite in the breakroom. But now? Now, the thought of eating just... it just turns my stomach, honestly. Most days, I'm lucky if I can force myself to choke down a cup of coffee and maybe a dry granola bar before I head out the door for a shift. And even that feels like a chore. It's like my stomach is just tied in knots, or like there's a rock sitting in it, and there's just no room for food. I look at a plate of food and I just feel nothing. No hunger, no craving, just... apathy, I guess.\n\nAnd it's really starting to show, too. I've noticed over the last few months that my scrubs are just hanging off me. They used to fit pretty snug, especially around the shoulders and waist, but now I'm constantly having to pull the drawstring tighter and tighter just to keep my pants up. I catch sight of myself in the mirror in the patient bathrooms sometimes, and my face just looks... gaunt. Thinner. And I haven't been trying to lose weight at all, not one bit. I just literally have zero desire to eat. So, no, definitely no increased appetite. Quite the opposite, really. It's just another thing that feels completely broken right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant weight loss recently, even though you haven’t been dieting or trying to lose weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly, like I was just saying a second ago. That's honestly the main thing that brings me in today, because the weight loss is getting impossible to ignore. I haven't actually stepped on a scale recently because I don't even own one, but it's definitely significant. \n\nLike I said, my eating has just gotten so far off track that some days I barely get down a cup of coffee and a granola bar before a whole twelve-hour shift. That's literally all I can manage to force down. I'm having to tie the drawstrings so tight that the fabric bunches up all weird around my waist, and even then I'm pulling them up all day. \n\nAnd I want to be really clear about this—I am not trying to lose weight at all. I've never been on a diet in my life, I've always been a pretty solid guy, you know? I'm not trying to slim down or watch my carbs or anything like that. The weight is just falling off me all on its own because my stomach just won't accept food anymore. It's just melting off, and I’m not doing a single thing to try and make it happen.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating or thinking clearly lately, like your mind feels foggy or you're struggling to focus on tasks?\n\nPatient: Concentrating? No... no, I wouldn't say that. It's actually kind of funny you ask that, because with how bad everything else is—the not sleeping, the weight falling off me, that constant heavy feeling I was telling you about—you'd think my brain would just completely turn to mush, right? But honestly, my focus is fine. My mind is probably the only thing that still feels like it's working the way it's supposed to. \n\nI mean, working on the floor at the hospital, you really can't afford to get foggy or lose your concentration. It's a safety issue. If I'm doing a two-person assist, or getting a fall-risk patient up to the commode, I have to be completely dialed in. I have to remember exactly what the charge nurse told me during handoff, like which side is the patient's weak side, or where their IV lines and catheters are so nothing gets pulled out. If anything, I'm almost hyper-aware of everything, especially of how miserable I feel doing it all. But the actual thinking part? Following instructions, finishing my rounds, paying attention to what people are saying to me? That's all completely intact. I just do the work, even if I don't care about it one bit anymore. So, yeah, no trouble focusing or thinking clearly. The engine is still running fine, I just... I just don't want to be driving the car, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems with your ability to think clearly, make decisions, or focus on tasks lately?\n\nPatient: I think... wait, didn't you just ask me that? About concentrating and thinking clearly? Because, yeah, like I was just saying, my focus is actually totally fine. I make the call instantly. There's no hesitation, no second-guessing myself, no standing there looking confused while someone falls on the floor. \n\nAnd it's the same thing with focusing on tasks. Whether I'm charting my vitals, emptying drains, or just figuring out the most efficient way to get through my morning rounds before breakfast trays arrive, I can map it all out in my head perfectly. I don't get sidetracked. I don't walk into a room and forget why I went in there. I'm just... I'm still sharp. The gears are turning exactly the way they're supposed to. \n\nIt's just that the whole time I'm doing these tasks, making these decisions, I'm just feeling this absolute, crushing emptiness. No, no, I've never had anything like that. I mean, I've had days where I felt good, you know, just normal good days before all this heavy stuff started, but never anything extreme where I was acting weird or out of character. I've always been a pretty level-headed guy, steady eddy, that's what they used to call me at work. 

I don't get super high or super low—well, except for right now with this low, obviously—but I mean, I don't get hyper or irritable or start doing crazy things. I've seen patients like that, where they come in and they're talking a mile a minute, bouncing off the walls, getting mad at everyone for no reason, or thinking they can fly or whatever. I know what that looks like, and that is absolutely not me. I've never been the type to go out and spend a bunch of money I don't have, or stay up all night painting the house, or get into fights with people because I'm just so on edge. 

Even when I'm stressed, like when we're short-staffed and I've got three fall risks on the same hall, I just put my head down and do the work. I might get a little quiet, but I don't snap at people. I don't change who I am. My sister, she's kind of a firecracker, she gets real irritable and you can tell when she's having a bad day because she just lets everyone have it. But me? No. I just keep to myself. So no, no periods of feeling overly good or irritable, nothing where people would look at me and say, "Whoa, what's gotten into him?" I'm just... I'm just me, just really, really worn down and empty right now. But my personality hasn't flipped or anything like that.

Now I just don't care. The fishing rods are just gathering dust in the garage. I haven't even washed my car in weeks, which is so unlike me. It’s like the color has just been drained out of everything. I try to watch TV or listen to the radio on my commute, but it all just feels like noise. There’s no joy in it anymore. I just feel so empty and sad all the time, and it’s just exhausting trying to pretend I'm okay when I'm at work, putting on that fake smile for the patients when inside I just feel completely dead.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping lately, like not being able to fall asleep, staying asleep, or maybe even sleeping too much?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sleep is... well, it's a whole other nightmare, honestly. I just sort of doze and wake up, doze and wake up, just constantly on edge. \n\nBut the absolute worst part is what happens a few hours later. I flip the pillow to the cool side, I try pulling the blankets up, kicking them off, trying to get comfortable, but I just can't do it. My mind is already racing again. So I just end up lying there for hours in the pitch black, agonizing over everything, just waiting and waiting for the alarm to finally go off at 5:30. By the time it actually rings, I feel worse than when I went to bed.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself or not wanting to live anymore?\n\nPatient: Well... it’s hard to say out loud, but... yeah, I have. I mean, I haven't made a plan or anything like that. I'm not sitting around figuring out how to, you know, actually do something to myself. I don't have a specific method in mind or a date picked out or anything concrete like that. But... the thoughts are definitely there. They just kind of creep in, especially during those early morning hours when I'm just lying there awake at 3 AM. \n\nIt’s more like... I just catch myself wishing I wouldn't wake up at all. Like, when I finally do drift off for those few miserable hours, part of me is just hoping that the alarm won't matter because I just won't open my eyes again. It just feels like it would be so much easier. You know? Like, if I could just disappear, just sort of fade away into nothing, then I wouldn't have to face another shift, I wouldn't have to put on that fake smile for the patients, I wouldn't have to deal with this crushing weight on my chest all day long. \n\nI think about it when I'm driving to work, too. I'll be sitting at a red light, watching the cars go by, and I'll just think, \"What if I just kept driving? What if I just drove until I ran out of gas and then just walked away and never came back?\" Or I'll think about how simple it would be if I just wasn't here anymore. Not that I want to cause my coworkers trouble or leave them short-staffed, because I know they rely on me, but the pain of just existing right now is so exhausting. It takes so much energy just to breathe sometimes. So, no, no actual plans to hurt myself, but... yeah, a lot of wishing I could just be gone. It’s just this constant, heavy thought that it would all be so much simpler if I just wasn't around to feel this way anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way? When did these feelings of sadness, loss of interest, and difficulty eating and sleeping start?\n\nPatient: Gosh, how long... it’s hard to track sometimes, because when you work the floor, the days just sort of bleed into one another. You know, you do your shifts, you come home completely drained, and time just slips by without you really noticing. But... if I really try to pin it down, this steady depression and the loss of interest, that all settled in later. I'd say it has been with me for roughly the past year and four months. \n\nYeah, about sixteen months of this. I remember at first I kept trying to rationalize it, you know? Telling myself it was just regular burnout from the unit being short-staffed, or just the physical toll of transferring heavy patients all day. I kept thinking, \"I just need a long weekend, I just need to catch up on my sleep and I'll be back to normal, I'll get back to tinkering with my car.\" But a long weekend doesn't fix it. The weeks just kept rolling by, and that heavy, hollow feeling in my chest never went away. \n\nA year and four months is a really long time to carry that around every single day. It just stretches on and on, month after month of waking up at 3 AM and dreading the alarm clock. And after a while, you start to forget what it even felt like to not be exhausted and sad all the time. It kind of becomes your new normal, as awful as that sounds. You just keep waiting for the cloud to finally break, and it just... it just never does.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced periods where your mood was unusually elevated, you felt extremely energetic, or you needed less sleep but still had lots of energy?\n\nPatient: Elevated? Energetic? No, no, definitely not. I mean, I wish I had periods where I felt energetic, honestly. That sounds like a dream compared to this. But no, I've never had anything like that. I've never had a time where I felt unusually happy or up, or where I was bouncing off the walls with energy. I don't think I've felt truly energetic in... well, I can't even remember. \n\nAnd needing less sleep? Like I was saying before, I *get* less sleep, but I definitely don't feel energized from it. I feel like a zombie. When I'm lying there awake at 3 AM, my body is just screaming for rest, but my brain won't let it happen. It's not like I'm waking up at that hour feeling refreshed and ready to tackle the day, like, \"Oh boy, let me go clean the whole house and reorganize the garage!\" It's just pure exhaustion. \n\nI see some of the younger nurses on the floor, you know, they work a double shift and then go out for drinks afterward, and I just look at them and think, \"How on earth do you do that?\" If I manage to get through my twelve hours without collapsing, it's a miracle. I drag myself to my car, I drag myself into the house, and I just collapse. There's no extra energy, no feeling \"high\" on life or anything like that. It's just a constant, steady drain. Just pushing through mud, every single day. So, no, nothing like what you're describing. Just this heavy, tired feeling that never goes away.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting at least two weeks where you felt depressed most of the day, nearly every day, or lost interest in almost all activities?\n\nPatient: Two weeks? Oh... doc, if it was just two weeks, I think I’d be throwing a parade right now. I mean, two weeks sounds like a vacation compared to what this is. Like I was saying before about the last sixteen months or so... for most of this whole stretch, I’ve been living with that heavy, hollow mood and loss of interest. It’s not just having a few bad weeks here and there, or, you know, getting a temporary case of the blues because work is stressing me out. It's... it's every single day. \n\nMost of the day, nearly every day... yeah, absolutely. It’s like a thick fog that just rolled in and refused to leave. From the second I manage to force myself out of bed and put my scrubs on, it's just there. This weight. And the things I used to care about? I just couldn't care less anymore. I used to love my days off, I'd get out in the garage, I'd watch the game, I'd actually want to be awake and doing things. Now, if I have a day off, I just sit there. I don't want to do anything. I don't have the drive to do anything. It's just this constant, hollow emptiness that sits right in the middle of my chest, no matter where I am or what I'm supposed to be doing. \n\nSo when you ask if it's been at least two weeks... I mean, I really wish I could say yes, it was just a rough couple of weeks back in October and then it cleared up and I moved on. But it hasn't cleared up. It’s just been this endless stretch of nothing, day in and day out, where everything just feels pointless and gray. It just doesn't stop.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced periods where you slept much more than usual or had an increased appetite, even if you weren’t hungry?\n\nPatient: Sleep more? No, definitely not sleeping more, like I mentioned earlier with the waking up at 3 AM. It's the opposite of sleeping more. And as for my appetite... well, it's funny you ask about increased appetite, because it's actually the complete opposite of that, too. That's actually a big part of what finally made me realize I needed to come in and talk to someone. My eating has just gotten so incredibly off track. \n\nI used to have a really hearty appetite, you know? You kind of have to when you're on your feet for a twelve-hour shift, lifting patients, running up and down the halls. I used to pack these huge lunches—sandwiches, pasta, you name it—and I'd eat every last bite in the breakroom. But now? Now, the thought of eating just... it just turns my stomach, honestly. Most days, I'm lucky if I can force myself to choke down a cup of coffee and maybe a dry granola bar before I head out the door for a shift. And even that feels like a chore. It's like my stomach is just tied in knots, or like there's a rock sitting in it, and there's just no room for food. I look at a plate of food and I just feel nothing. No hunger, no craving, just... apathy, I guess.\n\nAnd it's really starting to show, too. I've noticed over the last few months that my scrubs are just hanging off me. They used to fit pretty snug, especially around the shoulders and waist, but now I'm constantly having to pull the drawstring tighter and tighter just to keep my pants up. I catch sight of myself in the mirror in the patient bathrooms sometimes, and my face just looks... gaunt. Thinner. And I haven't been trying to lose weight at all, not one bit. I just literally have zero desire to eat. So, no, definitely no increased appetite. Quite the opposite, really. It's just another thing that feels completely broken right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant weight loss recently, even though you haven’t been dieting or trying to lose weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly, like I was just saying a second ago. That's honestly the main thing that brings me in today, because the weight loss is getting impossible to ignore. I haven't actually stepped on a scale recently because I don't even own one, but it's definitely significant. \n\nLike I said, my eating has just gotten so far off track that some days I barely get down a cup of coffee and a granola bar before a whole twelve-hour shift. That's literally all I can manage to force down. And when you're working on your feet all day on the floor, lifting people and moving heavy equipment, you burn a lot of energy, but I'm putting absolutely nothing back into the tank. I'm having to tie the drawstrings so tight that the fabric bunches up all weird around my waist, and even then I'm pulling them up all day. \n\nAnd I want to be really clear about this—I am not trying to lose weight at all. I've never been on a diet in my life, I've always been a pretty solid guy, you know? I'm not trying to slim down or watch my carbs or anything like that. The weight is just falling off me all on its own because my stomach just won't accept food anymore. It's just melting off, and I’m not doing a single thing to try and make it happen.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating or thinking clearly lately, like your mind feels foggy or you're struggling to focus on tasks?\n\nPatient: Concentrating? No... no, I wouldn't say that. I have to remember exactly what the charge nurse told me during handoff, like which side is the patient's weak side, or where their IV lines and catheters are so nothing gets pulled out. I'm still tracking everything perfectly. \n\nEven with the lack of sleep and the fact that I'm barely running on a single granola bar, my brain just pushes through the mechanics of the day. And when you're working on your feet all day on the floor, lifting people and moving heavy equipment, you burn a lot of energy, but I'm putting absolutely nothing back into the tank. So yeah, my scrubs have just been hanging completely loose on me. I'm having to tie the drawstrings so tight that the fabric bunches up all weird around my waist, and even then I'm pulling them up all day. \n\nAnd I want to be really clear about this—I am not trying to lose weight at all. I've never been on a diet in my life, I've always been a pretty solid guy, you know? I'm not trying to slim down or watch my carbs or anything like that. The weight is just falling off me all on its own because my stomach just won't accept food anymore. It's just melting off, and I’m not doing a single thing to try and make it happen.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating or thinking clearly lately, like your mind feels foggy or you're struggling to focus on tasks?\n\nPatient: Concentrating? No... no, I wouldn't say that. It's actually kind of funny you ask that, because with how bad everything else is—the not sleeping, the weight falling off me, that constant heavy feeling I was telling you about—you'd think my brain would just completely turn to mush, right? But honestly, my focus is fine. My mind is probably the only thing that still feels like it's working the way it's supposed to. \n\nI mean, working on the floor at the hospital, you really can't afford to get foggy or lose your concentration. It's a safety issue. If I'm doing a two-person assist, or getting a fall-risk patient up to the commode, I have to be completely dialed in. I have to remember exactly what the charge nurse told me during handoff, like which side is the patient's weak side, or where their IV lines and catheters are so nothing gets pulled out. And I haven't messed any of that up. Not once. I'm still tracking everything perfectly. \n\nEven with the lack of sleep and the fact that I'm barely running on a single granola bar, my brain just pushes through the mechanics of the day. I can remember that the guy in room 412 needs his ice water refilled, or that the bed alarm in 410 is super sensitive and needs to be reset a specific way. It's not foggy at all up there. If anything, I'm almost hyper-aware of everything, especially of how miserable I feel doing it all. But the actual thinking part? Following instructions, finishing my rounds, paying attention to what people are saying to me? That's all completely intact. I just do the work, even if I don't care about it one bit anymore. So, yeah, no trouble focusing or thinking clearly. The engine is still running fine, I just... I just don't want to be driving the car, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems with your ability to think clearly, make decisions, or focus on tasks lately?\n\nPatient: I think... wait, didn't you just ask me that? About concentrating and thinking clearly? Because, yeah, like I was just saying, my focus is actually totally fine. Making decisions, thinking clearly, focusing on tasks—it's all the same as it's always been. \n\nI know it probably seems strange, especially since I just told you how I'm basically running on fumes, skipping meals, and waking up at 3 AM staring at the ceiling for hours. You'd think my brain would be completely fried, right? Like I wouldn't be able to put two sentences together or figure out what to do next. But honestly, making decisions hasn't been an issue at all. Like at work, if a patient suddenly starts sliding down in their chair and I have to decide in a split second whether to grab them by the gait belt or call for backup, my brain just snaps to it. I make the call instantly. There's no hesitation, no second-guessing myself, no standing there looking confused while someone falls on the floor. \n\nAnd it's the same thing with focusing on tasks. Whether I'm charting my vitals, emptying drains, or just figuring out the most efficient way to get through my morning rounds before breakfast trays arrive, I can map it all out in my head perfectly. I don't get sidetracked. I don't walk into a room and forget why I went in there. I'm just... I'm still sharp. The gears are turning exactly the way they're supposed to. \n\nIt's just that the whole time I'm doing these tasks, making these decisions, I'm just feeling this absolute, crushing emptiness. Like, I know *how* to do everything, and I *can* do everything, but I just don't see the point anymore. But the actual ability to think? To decide what needs to be done and focus on doing it? No, no problems there. Like I said, it's probably the only part of me that isn't falling apart right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt so good or so irritable that people around you noticed a change in your behavior, or where you were doing things that were out of character for you?\n\nPatient: No, no, I've never had anything like that. I mean, I've had days where I felt good, you know, just normal good days before all this heavy stuff started, but never anything extreme where I was acting weird or out of character. I've always been a pretty level-headed guy, steady eddy, that's what they used to call me at work. \n\nI don't get super high or super low—well, except for right now with this low, obviously—but I mean, I don't get hyper or irritable or start doing crazy things. I've seen patients like that, where they come in and they're talking a mile a minute, bouncing off the walls, getting mad at everyone for no reason, or thinking they can fly or whatever. I know what that looks like, and that is absolutely not me. I've never been the type to go out and spend a bunch of money I don't have, or stay up all night painting the house, or get into fights with people because I'm just so on edge. \n\nEven when I'm stressed, like when we're short-staffed and I've got three fall risks on the same hall, I just put my head down and do the work. I might get a little quiet, but I don't snap at people. I don't change who I am. My sister, she's kind of a firecracker, she gets real irritable and you can tell when she's having a bad day because she just lets everyone have it. But me? No. I just keep to myself. So no, no periods of feeling overly good or irritable, nothing where people would look at me and say, \"Whoa, what's gotten into him?\" I'm just... I'm just me, just really, really worn down and empty right now. But my personality hasn't flipped or anything like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, that’s... that’s exactly it. It’s like someone just unplugged me from everything I used to care about. I used to be a pretty active guy on my days off, you know? I’d go up to Van Cortlandt Park—it’s a great park, lots of cross-country trails, really gets you out of that clinical, sterile headspace of the hospital without actually having to leave the city. I’d go running there all the time. It was the best way to burn off the stress from being on my feet all week. And I’d always bring my camera along, too. I really loved taking photos. Nothing professional, just, you know, catching the light through the trees or the old stone bridges, maybe some wildlife. It brought me a lot of peace. And on the weekends, I had this regular thing where I’d meet up with some friends and we’d play chess. We’d sit around for hours, drinking coffee, strategizing, talking trash, just enjoying the game. I looked forward to it every single week.

But now? I haven't done any of that in... I don't even know how long. I look at my running shoes sitting by the door, or I see my camera on the desk just gathering dust, and I just feel... absolutely nothing. I actually tell myself, "There’s no point." Like, why bother? What is running going to do? What is taking a picture of a tree going to change about anything? It all just feels completely pointless. My friends will text me to see if I want to play a few games of chess, and I just ignore them or make up an excuse about being tired from my shifts. The thought of sitting there and trying to care about moving pieces on a board just sounds impossible. It doesn't sound fun, it doesn't sound relaxing, it just sounds like a chore.

So instead, I just stay in my apartment. I’ll sit on the couch for hours and just scroll on my phone. Not even looking at anything specific or reading anything interesting, just mindlessly swiping through garbage because it takes zero emotional effort. Or, and this is probably the most depressing part, I’ll open up my laptop and just sit there checking my budget spreadsheet. I have this Excel file where I track my rent and my groceries and my bills, and I’ll just open it and stare at the numbers. I'm not even really doing any math or fixing anything, just checking it, scrolling up and down the rows, looking at the columns over and over again because it’s totally mindless and I don't have to feel anything while I do it. That’s my whole life now outside of work. No park, no photos, no chess. Just staring at a screen and telling myself there's no point in trying to do anything else.

But even knowing all that, knowing people need me, I just feel so incredibly down. \n\nIt’s not just a passing sadness, either. It’s this deep, dark cloud that just follows me everywhere. I used to love my days off. I used to go fishing down by the lake, or just tinker with my car in the driveway, you know, normal stuff. I used to look forward to the weekend so much. But now? Now I just don't care. The fishing rods are just gathering dust in the garage. I haven't even washed my car in weeks, which is so unlike me. It’s like the color has just been drained out of everything. I try to watch TV or listen to the radio on my commute, but it all just feels like noise. There’s no joy in it anymore. I just feel so empty and sad all the time, and it’s just exhausting trying to pretend I'm okay when I'm at work, putting on that fake smile for the patients when inside I just feel completely dead.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping lately, like not being able to fall asleep, staying asleep, or maybe even sleeping too much?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sleep is... well, it's a whole other nightmare, honestly. I definitely don't sleep too much, it's the exact opposite. I mean, you'd think after a twelve-hour shift on my feet, wrestling with heavy equipment and helping patients all day, I'd just pass out the second my head hits the pillow. My body is completely exhausted, my back aches, my feet throb, but I get into bed and... my brain just refuses to shut off. \n\nMy sleep is just so incredibly broken up. I just lie there in the dark, staring at the ceiling, and I start replaying the entire workday in my head. Like, did I remember to record Mrs. Gable's fluid intake? Did I lock the linens cart on the third floor? Should I have told the charge nurse about how Mr. Miller was transferring? It’s this endless, exhausting loop of everything I did or didn't do. \n\nAnd while I'm doing that, I'm also just lying there completely tense, listening for noises. Every little sound gets magnified in the quiet. The house settling, the fridge kicking on, a branch brushing the siding... I just fixate on it, waiting for the next sound, so I never really sink into a deep sleep. I just sort of doze and wake up, doze and wake up, just constantly on edge. \n\nBut the absolute worst part is what happens a few hours later. Even if I do finally manage to string together a little bit of actual sleep, my eyes will just snap wide open. I'll look over at the clock on the nightstand, and it is always, without fail, right around 3 or 4 in the morning. Just staring at those red numbers glaring in the dark. And once I'm up at that hour, that's it. I am completely done. I try so hard to settle back down. I flip the pillow to the cool side, I try pulling the blankets up, kicking them off, trying to get comfortable, but I just can't do it. My mind is already racing again. So I just end up lying there for hours in the pitch black, agonizing over everything, just waiting and waiting for the alarm to finally go off at 5:30. By the time it actually rings, I feel worse than when I went to bed.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself or not wanting to live anymore?\n\nPatient: Well... it’s hard to say out loud, but... yeah, I have. I mean, I haven't made a plan or anything like that. I'm not sitting around figuring out how to, you know, actually do something to myself. I don't have a specific method in mind or a date picked out or anything concrete like that. But... the thoughts are definitely there. They just kind of creep in, especially during those early morning hours when I'm just lying there awake at 3 AM. \n\nIt’s more like... I just catch myself wishing I wouldn't wake up at all. Like, when I finally do drift off for those few miserable hours, part of me is just hoping that the alarm won't matter because I just won't open my eyes again. It just feels like it would be so much easier. You know? Like, if I could just disappear, just sort of fade away into nothing, then I wouldn't have to face another shift, I wouldn't have to put on that fake smile for the patients, I wouldn't have to deal with this crushing weight on my chest all day long. \n\nI think about it when I'm driving to work, too. I'll be sitting at a red light, watching the cars go by, and I'll just think, \"What if I just kept driving? What if I just drove until I ran out of gas and then just walked away and never came back?\" Or I'll think about how simple it would be if I just wasn't here anymore. Not that I want to cause my coworkers trouble or leave them short-staffed, because I know they rely on me, but the pain of just existing right now is so exhausting. It takes so much energy just to breathe sometimes. So, no, no actual plans to hurt myself, but... yeah, a lot of wishing I could just be gone. It’s just this constant, heavy thought that it would all be so much simpler if I just wasn't around to feel this way anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way? When did these feelings of sadness, loss of interest, and difficulty eating and sleeping start?\n\nPatient: Gosh, how long... it’s hard to track sometimes, because when you work the floor, the days just sort of bleed into one another. You know, you do your shifts, you come home completely drained, and time just slips by without you really noticing. But... if I really try to pin it down, this steady depression and the loss of interest, that all settled in later. I'd say it has been with me for roughly the past year and four months. \n\nYeah, about sixteen months of this. I remember at first I kept trying to rationalize it, you know? Telling myself it was just regular burnout from the unit being short-staffed, or just the physical toll of transferring heavy patients all day. I kept thinking, \"I just need a long weekend, I just need to catch up on my sleep and I'll be back to normal, I'll get back to tinkering with my car.\" But a long weekend doesn't fix it. The weeks just kept rolling by, and that heavy, hollow feeling in my chest never went away. \n\nA year and four months is a really long time to carry that around every single day. It just stretches on and on, month after month of waking up at 3 AM and dreading the alarm clock. And after a while, you start to forget what it even felt like to not be exhausted and sad all the time. It kind of becomes your new normal, as awful as that sounds. You just keep waiting for the cloud to finally break, and it just... it just never does.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced periods where your mood was unusually elevated, you felt extremely energetic, or you needed less sleep but still had lots of energy?\n\nPatient: Elevated? Energetic? No, no, definitely not. I mean, I wish I had periods where I felt energetic, honestly. That sounds like a dream compared to this. But no, I've never had anything like that. I've never had a time where I felt unusually happy or up, or where I was bouncing off the walls with energy. I don't think I've felt truly energetic in... well, I can't even remember. \n\nAnd needing less sleep? Like I was saying before, I *get* less sleep, but I definitely don't feel energized from it. I feel like a zombie. When I'm lying there awake at 3 AM, my body is just screaming for rest, but my brain won't let it happen. It's not like I'm waking up at that hour feeling refreshed and ready to tackle the day, like, \"Oh boy, let me go clean the whole house and reorganize the garage!\" It's just pure exhaustion. \n\nI see some of the younger nurses on the floor, you know, they work a double shift and then go out for drinks afterward, and I just look at them and think, \"How on earth do you do that?\" If I manage to get through my twelve hours without collapsing, it's a miracle. I drag myself to my car, I drag myself into the house, and I just collapse. There's no extra energy, no feeling \"high\" on life or anything like that. It's just a constant, steady drain. Just pushing through mud, every single day. So, no, nothing like what you're describing. Just this heavy, tired feeling that never goes away.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting at least two weeks where you felt depressed most of the day, nearly every day, or lost interest in almost all activities?\n\nPatient: Two weeks? Oh... doc, if it was just two weeks, I think I’d be throwing a parade right now. I mean, two weeks sounds like a vacation compared to what this is. Like I was saying before about the last sixteen months or so... for most of this whole stretch, I’ve been living with that heavy, hollow mood and loss of interest. It’s not just having a few bad weeks here and there, or, you know, getting a temporary case of the blues because work is stressing me out. It's... it's every single day. \n\nMost of the day, nearly every day... yeah, absolutely. It’s like a thick fog that just rolled in and refused to leave. From the second I manage to force myself out of bed and put my scrubs on, it's just there. This weight. And the things I used to care about? I just couldn't care less anymore. I used to love my days off, I'd get out in the garage, I'd watch the game, I'd actually want to be awake and doing things. Now, if I have a day off, I just sit there. I don't want to do anything. I don't have the drive to do anything. It's just this constant, hollow emptiness that sits right in the middle of my chest, no matter where I am or what I'm supposed to be doing. \n\nSo when you ask if it's been at least two weeks... I mean, I really wish I could say yes, it was just a rough couple of weeks back in October and then it cleared up and I moved on. But it hasn't cleared up. It’s just been this endless stretch of nothing, day in and day out, where everything just feels pointless and gray. It just doesn't stop.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced periods where you slept much more than usual or had an increased appetite, even if you weren’t hungry?\n\nPatient: Sleep more? No, definitely not sleeping more, like I mentioned earlier with the waking up at 3 AM. It's the opposite of sleeping more. And as for my appetite... well, it's funny you ask about increased appetite, because it's actually the complete opposite of that, too. That's actually a big part of what finally made me realize I needed to come in and talk to someone. My eating has just gotten so incredibly off track. \n\nI used to have a really hearty appetite, you know? You kind of have to when you're on your feet for a twelve-hour shift, lifting patients, running up and down the halls. I used to pack these huge lunches—sandwiches, pasta, you name it—and I'd eat every last bite in the breakroom. But now? Now, the thought of eating just... it just turns my stomach, honestly. Most days, I'm lucky if I can force myself to choke down a cup of coffee and maybe a dry granola bar before I head out the door for a shift. And even that feels like a chore. It's like my stomach is just tied in knots, or like there's a rock sitting in it, and there's just no room for food. I look at a plate of food and I just feel nothing. No hunger, no craving, just... apathy, I guess.\n\nAnd it's really starting to show, too. I've noticed over the last few months that my scrubs are just hanging off me. They used to fit pretty snug, especially around the shoulders and waist, but now I'm constantly having to pull the drawstring tighter and tighter just to keep my pants up. I catch sight of myself in the mirror in the patient bathrooms sometimes, and my face just looks... gaunt. Thinner. And I haven't been trying to lose weight at all, not one bit. I just literally have zero desire to eat. So, no, definitely no increased appetite. Quite the opposite, really. It's just another thing that feels completely broken right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant weight loss recently, even though you haven’t been dieting or trying to lose weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly, like I was just saying a second ago. That's honestly the main thing that brings me in today, because the weight loss is getting impossible to ignore. I haven't actually stepped on a scale recently because I don't even own one, but it's definitely significant. \n\nLike I said, my eating has just gotten so far off track that some days I barely get down a cup of coffee and a granola bar before a whole twelve-hour shift. That's literally all I can manage to force down. And when you're working on your feet all day on the floor, lifting people and moving heavy equipment, you burn a lot of energy, but I'm putting absolutely nothing back into the tank. So yeah, my scrubs have just been hanging completely loose on me. I'm having to tie the drawstrings so tight that the fabric bunches up all weird around my waist, and even then I'm pulling them up all day. \n\nAnd I want to be really clear about this—I am not trying to lose weight at all. I've never been on a diet in my life, I've always been a pretty solid guy, you know? I'm not trying to slim down or watch my carbs or anything like that. The weight is just falling off me all on its own because my stomach just won't accept food anymore. It's just melting off, and I’m not doing a single thing to try and make it happen.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating or thinking clearly lately, like your mind feels foggy or you're struggling to focus on tasks?\n\nPatient: Concentrating? No... no, I wouldn't say that. It's actually kind of funny you ask that, because with how bad everything else is—the not sleeping, the weight falling off me, that constant heavy feeling I was telling you about—you'd think my brain would just completely turn to mush, right? But honestly, my focus is fine. My mind is probably the only thing that still feels like it's working the way it's supposed to. \n\nI mean, working on the floor at the hospital, you really can't afford to get foggy or lose your concentration. It's a safety issue. If I'm doing a two-person assist, or getting a fall-risk patient up to the commode, I have to be completely dialed in. I have to remember exactly what the charge nurse told me during handoff, like which side is the patient's weak side, or where their IV lines and catheters are so nothing gets pulled out. And I haven't messed any of that up. Not once. I'm still tracking everything perfectly. \n\nEven with the lack of sleep and the fact that I'm barely running on a single granola bar, my brain just pushes through the mechanics of the day. I can remember that the guy in room 412 needs his ice water refilled, or that the bed alarm in 410 is super sensitive and needs to be reset a specific way. It's not foggy at all up there. If anything, I'm almost hyper-aware of everything, especially of how miserable I feel doing it all. But the actual thinking part? Following instructions, finishing my rounds, paying attention to what people are saying to me? That's all completely intact. I just do the work, even if I don't care about it one bit anymore. So, yeah, no trouble focusing or thinking clearly. The engine is still running fine, I just... I just don't want to be driving the car, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems with your ability to think clearly, make decisions, or focus on tasks lately?\n\nPatient: I think... wait, didn't you just ask me that? About concentrating and thinking clearly? Because, yeah, like I was just saying, my focus is actually totally fine. Making decisions, thinking clearly, focusing on tasks—it's all the same as it's always been. \n\nI know it probably seems strange, especially since I just told you how I'm basically running on fumes, skipping meals, and waking up at 3 AM staring at the ceiling for hours. You'd think my brain would be completely fried, right? Like I wouldn't be able to put two sentences together or figure out what to do next. But honestly, making decisions hasn't been an issue at all. Like at work, if a patient suddenly starts sliding down in their chair and I have to decide in a split second whether to grab them by the gait belt or call for backup, my brain just snaps to it. I make the call instantly. There's no hesitation, no second-guessing myself, no standing there looking confused while someone falls on the floor. \n\nAnd it's the same thing with focusing on tasks. Whether I'm charting my vitals, emptying drains, or just figuring out the most efficient way to get through my morning rounds before breakfast trays arrive, I can map it all out in my head perfectly. I don't get sidetracked. I don't walk into a room and forget why I went in there. I'm just... I'm still sharp. The gears are turning exactly the way they're supposed to. \n\nIt's just that the whole time I'm doing these tasks, making these decisions, I'm just feeling this absolute, crushing emptiness. Like, I know *how* to do everything, and I *can* do everything, but I just don't see the point anymore. But the actual ability to think? To decide what needs to be done and focus on doing it? No, no problems there. Like I said, it's probably the only part of me that isn't falling apart right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt so good or so irritable that people around you noticed a change in your behavior, or where you were doing things that were out of character for you?\n\nPatient: No, no, I've never had anything like that. I mean, I've had days where I felt good, you know, just normal good days before all this heavy stuff started, but never anything extreme where I was acting weird or out of character. I've always been a pretty level-headed guy, steady eddy, that's what they used to call me at work. \n\nI don't get super high or super low—well, except for right now with this low, obviously—but I mean, I don't get hyper or irritable or start doing crazy things. I've seen patients like that, where they come in and they're talking a mile a minute, bouncing off the walls, getting mad at everyone for no reason, or thinking they can fly or whatever. I know what that looks like, and that is absolutely not me. I've never been the type to go out and spend a bunch of money I don't have, or stay up all night painting the house, or get into fights with people because I'm just so on edge. \n\nEven when I'm stressed, like when we're short-staffed and I've got three fall risks on the same hall, I just put my head down and do the work. I might get a little quiet, but I don't snap at people. I don't change who I am. My sister, she's kind of a firecracker, she gets real irritable and you can tell when she's having a bad day because she just lets everyone have it. But me? No. I just keep to myself. So no, no periods of feeling overly good or irritable, nothing where people would look at me and say, \"Whoa, what's gotten into him?\" I'm just... I'm just me, just really, really worn down and empty right now. But my personality hasn't flipped or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt a complete loss of pleasure in all or almost all activities, or found that even things that used to bring you happiness no longer affect you at all?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s... that’s exactly it. It’s like someone just unplugged me from everything I used to care about. I used to be a pretty active guy on my days off, you know? I’d go up to Van Cortlandt Park—it’s a great park, lots of cross-country trails, really gets you out of that clinical, sterile headspace of the hospital without actually having to leave the city. I’d go running there all the time. It was the best way to burn off the stress from being on my feet all week. And I’d always bring my camera along, too. I really loved taking photos. Nothing professional, just, you know, catching the light through the trees or the old stone bridges, maybe some wildlife. It brought me a lot of peace. And on the weekends, I had this regular thing where I’d meet up with some friends and we’d play chess. We’d sit around for hours, drinking coffee, strategizing, talking trash, just enjoying the game. I looked forward to it every single week.\n\nBut now? I haven't done any of that in... I don't even know how long. I look at my running shoes sitting by the door, or I see my camera on the desk just gathering dust, and I just feel... absolutely nothing. I actually tell myself, \"There’s no point.\" Like, why bother? What is running going to do? What is taking a picture of a tree going to change about anything? It all just feels completely pointless. My friends will text me to see if I want to play a few games of chess, and I just ignore them or make up an excuse about being tired from my shifts. The thought of sitting there and trying to care about moving pieces on a board just sounds impossible. It doesn't sound fun, it doesn't sound relaxing, it just sounds like a chore.\n\nSo instead, I just stay in my apartment. I’ll sit on the couch for hours and just scroll on my phone. Not even looking at anything specific or reading anything interesting, just mindlessly swiping through garbage because it takes zero emotional effort. Or, and this is probably the most depressing part, I’ll open up my laptop and just sit there checking my budget spreadsheet. I have this Excel file where I track my rent and my groceries and my bills, and I’ll just open it and stare at the numbers. I'm not even really doing any math or fixing anything, just checking it, scrolling up and down the rows, looking at the columns over and over again because it’s totally mindless and I don't have to feel anything while I do it. That’s my whole life now outside of work. No park, no photos, no chess. Just staring at a screen and telling myself there's no point in trying to do anything else."
